58:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but using them on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. A natural improvement over today's single-head designs is a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head for each input window. We show that this natural design catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: the router collapses to a single expert and the remaining experts are never used. The cause, which we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, is that instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Standard MoE rescue mechanisms---load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, and expert-choice---do not recover performance, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of where collapse occurs, including boundary cases where it does not. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins on every dataset/freeze-level cell of the primary backbone, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} that we verify empirically: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse, so frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.

1	58:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but using them on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. A natural improvement over today's single-head designs is a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head for each input window. We show that this natural design catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: the router collapses to a single expert and the remaining experts are never used. The cause, which we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, is that instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Standard MoE rescue mechanisms---load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, and expert-choice---do not recover performance, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of where collapse occurs, including boundary cases where it does not. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins on every dataset/freeze-level cell of the primary backbone, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} that we verify empirically: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse, so frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.
2	64:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter---more than the backbone choice---determines deployment utility.
3	66:Today, every public TSFM benchmark uses a single static head that pools hidden states and projects them to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. Real time series are not so uniform: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical---one TSFM in GPU memory can serve thousands of tenants through small ($\sim$1.7\,MB) per-task adapters swapped in microseconds, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
4	68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach catastrophically fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms---load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice---also do not help (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
5	75:\item We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition (Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) that yields a signal-ratio predictor of which datasets benefit from raw routing---including boundary cases where they do not---and validate the same principle across three normalization families, five backbones, and a vision modality.
6	220:\caption{\textbf{Problem overview.} \textbf{(a)}~Today's TSFM benchmarks adapt the frozen backbone with a \emph{single static head} that pools hidden states for every input window, regardless of the window's per-sample statistics. \textbf{(b)}~The natural extension---a mixture of expert adapters with a learned router---catastrophically fails on instance-normalized TSFMs: routing collapses to a single expert (entropy $0.000{\pm}0.000$), and the remaining experts receive no gradient. Both panels share the same backbone; only the head changes.}
7	227:\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data---LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; benchmarking work includes TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} and GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval}. All of these search over finetuning \textit{strategies} while keeping a single adapter architecture. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window.
8	231:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse is a well-known failure mode addressed by load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}; AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \emph{input} carries discriminative information. Sweeping the five families across eleven configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs---AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}---supports this view: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
9	233:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes catastrophic routing collapse---a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature.}

A second architecture introduced below---\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA), in which each expert has its own sigmoid gate on raw input rather than a shared external router---independently replicates this pattern (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). On ETTh1, Random Forest regression on raw input alone reduces MSE by $44\%$ versus Ridge, revealing substantial nonlinear temporal structure in the raw data. However, Random Forest on backbone features adds only $+0.02\%$ to the linear baseline---the encoder destroys this nonlinear structure entirely. Few-shot experiments (Table~\ref{tab:fewshot}) confirm the pattern holds at every training set size $N \in \{10, \ldots, 5000\}$: DLinear dominates at all $N$, ruling out data scarcity as a regime where the frozen backbone recovers its advantage. The gap to DLinear reflects information destruction by the normalization--encoding pipeline, not a limitation of adapter design or training data volume.
55	1945:\caption{\textbf{Extension to a native-MoE backbone: Moirai-MoE} (strictly frozen, Top-2 sparse, H=96, 5 seeds, 60 runs total). Moirai-MoE is a TSFM whose internal FFN layers are themselves sparsely-gated experts~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}, with LayerNorm only (no RevIN). RR-MoA wins \textbf{all 6 datasets} by $-37\%$ to $-90\%$, confirming that adapter-level routing stacks on top of backbone-internal routing. AdaMix's hidden-state routing does \emph{not} collapse on Moirai-MoE --- routing entropy is exactly $\log K = 1.6094$ to four decimal places across all 30 cells ($\sigma{=}0.0000$), indicating mathematically uniform routing --- yet AdaMix still only matches the best fixed baseline MSE, demonstrating that raw-signal routing is information-richer than hidden-state routing even when the hidden states retain enough signal to avoid collapse.}
56	2000: & \cellcolor{gray!8} DLinear (from scratch) & \cellcolor{gray!8} $0.149 \pm 0.017$ & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- & & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- \\
57	2005: & \cellcolor{gray!8} DLinear (from scratch) & \cellcolor{gray!8} $0.204 \pm 0.002$ & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- & & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- \\
58	2015:\textbf{(i) Multi-tenant cloud serving.} A single TSFM sits in GPU VRAM and serves thousands of tenants via lightweight, hot-swappable per-tenant adapters in host RAM. Per-tenant DLinear requires collecting, storing, and periodically retraining on each tenant's historical data ($O(N)$ training pipelines for $N$ tenants), whereas the frozen backbone is loaded once and adapter weights (${\sim}50$--$500$K parameters, ${\sim}0.2$--$2$\,MB) are swapped from host RAM in microseconds. At $N{=}1{,}000$ tenants, the adapter approach eliminates $1{,}000$ independent training jobs and enables instant onboarding of new tenants via zero-shot transfer---capabilities absent from per-tenant supervised baselines.
59	2026:\caption{\textbf{Inference latency and memory} (MOMENT-small, A10G GPU, batch size 128). RR-MoA Top-2 adds $4.3$\,ms ($+10\%$) latency and $2.6$\,MB peak memory over a single adapter---negligible compared to the backbone forward pass.}
60	2033:Backbone only & $44.3$ & $374$ & --- \\
61	2042:The RR-MoA routing overhead ($+4.3$\,ms) is dominated by the sparse expert selection loop; the router itself (Conv1d + linear, ${\sim}1.1$K params) adds $<0.1$\,ms. At deployment scale, adapter swapping between tenants requires loading $426$K parameters (${\sim}1.7$\,MB) from host RAM---orders of magnitude cheaper than reloading a $35$M parameter backbone. At $N{=}100$ tenants, adapter-swapping throughput is $141$ tenants/sec. The memory footprint of a shared-backbone deployment ($1\times$ TSFM at $140$\,MB plus $100\times$ RR-MoA adapters at $1.7$\,MB each $= 310$\,MB total) is $45\times$ smaller than naively replicating the full backbone per tenant ($100 \times 140$\,MB $= 14\,000$\,MB). \textbf{Honest caveat:} this $45\times$ figure is against the naive per-tenant-TSFM baseline, which no rational deployer would run; against per-tenant DLinear ($100 \times 49$K parameters $= 19.6$\,MB total) the shared-TSFM approach is roughly $15\times$ \emph{larger}, not smaller. The memory argument for TSFMs over DLinear is therefore not the core motivation --- that role is played by cross-task reuse, zero-shot onboarding, and the elimination of $O(N)$ per-tenant training pipelines (see \S\ref{sec:intro} and Appendix~\ref{app:deployment}).
62	2072:The best extended schedule (50ep cosine + layer-wise LR decay) improves over the 15-epoch baseline by only $4$--$5\%$, while frozen RR-MoA maintains a $33$--$41\%$ advantage. \textbf{Stronger optimization does not help.} We additionally swept 10 aggressive configurations at 100 epochs with $\text{lr}{\in}\{10^{-5}, 10^{-6}\}$, cosine schedule with 10-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay ($\gamma{=}0.8$), weight decay ${\in}\{0.001, 0.01, 0.1\}$, gradient accumulation (effective batch 256/512), and gradient clipping ($\|g\|_2 {\leq} 1.0$) --- 90 total configurations across 3 datasets $\times$ 3 seeds. The best per-dataset results (ETTh1: $1.030$, ETTm1: $0.877$, Weather: $0.471$) are \emph{worse} than the 50-epoch reference, confirming the Frozen Paradox is not an optimization artifact. The gap to frozen RR-MoA remains $51$--$71\%$.
63	2074:\textbf{Causal proof via RevIN ablation.} Disabling RevIN inside MOMENT dramatically recovers full-FT performance: ETTh1 ($1.063 \to 0.490$, $-54\%$), ETTm1 ($0.910 \to 0.364$, $-60\%$), Weather ($0.481 \to 0.187$, $-61\%$). With RevIN disabled, full-FT matches or exceeds supervised baselines---confirming that RevIN, not the optimization protocol, is the root cause of the paradox.
64	2101:DLinear (scratch) & $\mathit{0.416}$ & $\mathit{0.322}$ & $\mathit{0.208}$ & --- \\
65	2111:\textbf{Result.} Dual-stream experts reduce the average DLinear gap from $+57\%$ to $+5\%$---an $11\times$ reduction---and \emph{beat} DLinear on Weather ($0.198$ vs $0.208$, $p{=}0.03$, paired $t$-test). The learned blend parameters $\alpha_k \approx 0.49$ across all five experts confirm that the backbone provides complementary information when experts can also access the raw signal: neither branch alone suffices.
66	2113:\textbf{Extension to ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity.} Running all four variants on the three remaining forecasting datasets yields consistent gap closure (Table~\ref{tab:gap_closing_ext}). Dual-stream wins on Electricity at $+8\%$ and matches well on ETTm2 ($+26\%$), while \textbf{multi-resolution wins on ETTh2 at $+7.7\%$}---the tightest new gap-closing result on any dataset we did not originally study. Multi-resolution also performs competitively on ETTm2 ($+12\%$) and Electricity ($+13\%$), indicating the optimal architecture is dataset-dependent. FiLM remains consistently worst ($+48$--$147\%$), confirming that re-injecting only $(\mu,\sigma)$ is insufficient---the experts need access to the full raw signal, not just the stripped statistics.
67	2117:\caption{\textbf{Gap-closing extended to ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity} (MOMENT-small, strictly frozen, Top-2, 3 seeds, H=96, 15 epochs). Bold: best gap-closing variant per dataset. \textbf{Multi-resolution wins on ETTh2 at $+7.7\%$}---the closest new gap-closing result.}
68	2134:\textbf{Why this matters for the diagnosis.} The fact that the gap closes precisely when experts recover the information our diagnosis identified as destroyed---and remains open when they cannot (FiLM re-injects only $\mu,\sigma$, yielding $+48\%$ on ETTh1 vs.\ $+9\%$ for dual-stream)---is direct evidence that the diagnosis is both \emph{correct} and \emph{actionable}. The normalization--encoding bottleneck is not merely an observation; it is the causal mechanism whose resolution closes the gap. Importantly, the frozen backbone and raw-routed paradigm remain unchanged---these variants extend the \emph{expert architecture}, not the routing mechanism, confirming that the routing diagnosis (the core contribution of this paper) is orthogonal to expert design.
69	2136:\textbf{Expert pool ablation: canonical vs.\ macro pool.} To test whether RR-MoA's benefits depend on the specific choice of 5 canonical pooling heads, we replace the canonical pool with 5 AAS-discovered \emph{macro motifs} (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}) and rerun RR-MoA across all 6 forecasting datasets. Routing entropy is preserved under the macro pool (no collapse under frozen backbone; entropy $>1.3$ across all configurations), confirming that the collapse-prevention mechanism is \emph{pool-independent}. Absolute MSEs (frozen, Top-2, 3 seeds): ETTh1 $0.764{\pm}0.015$, ETTh2 $0.667{\pm}0.069$, ETTm1 $0.583{\pm}0.075$, ETTm2 $0.844{\pm}0.241$, Weather $0.274{\pm}0.024$, Electricity $0.401{\pm}0.039$. The canonical pool is stronger by $8$--$24\%$ on the three datasets where we have both numbers, indicating that while RR-MoA generalizes across diverse pool designs, the canonical heads are better tuned for the forecasting benchmark---the routing architecture is the orthogonal contribution.
70	2288:\textbf{Diagnostic: gates fail to learn.} We instrumented the per-sample gate mechanism to log its mean activation per expert per dataset. Across every configuration tested---\{rh128, rh192, rh256, rh320, rh384, rh512\} $\times$ \{d1, d2, d3 raw-branch depth\} $\times$ \{lr, wd, cosine, warmup\} hyperparameter combinations---the learned gates consistently plateau at $\sigma(0.0){\approx}0.5$:

72	2328:\textbf{Average gap}          & --- & --- & $\mathbf{+1.6\%}$ & --- & \textbf{4/6 match-or-beat} \\
73	2367:\textbf{Phase~1 --- hypothesis falsification.}
74	2369:\emph{Shared raw branch succeeds.} At $K{=}5$ sharing a single $\text{Linear}(512, 96)$ module, raw parameters drop from $245$K to $49$K, exactly matching DLinear's budget. ETTh1 closes from $+5.1\%$ (sig loss) to $+1.6\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.086$); ETTh2 closes from $+10.6\%$ (sig loss) to $+7.0\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.078$). Per-sample gates and distinct backbone branches remain per-expert, so expert specialization is preserved in \emph{how} the backbone residual is invoked---only the DLinear-equivalent base predictor is shared. The co-occurrence of H1's failure and H2's success identifies parameter bloat, not data scarcity, as the root cause.
75	2373:\textbf{Phase~2 --- orthogonal refinements.}
76	2374:\emph{Non-stationarity (Batch~4).} Hourly ETT data exhibits pronounced per-window level drift---the train/test windows have different means. Standard LTSF practice addresses this with NLinear: $\mathbf{y} = \text{Linear}(\mathbf{x} - x_T) + x_T$, where $x_T$ is a gradient-free anchor (the last input value). The linear layer is thereby constrained to model \emph{relative motion}, not absolute level. Testing \texttt{--raw-arch nlinear} as a drop-in replacement reduces ETTh1 from $+5.1\%$ to $+3.3\%$ and ETTh2 from $+10.6\%$ to $+6.9\%$ at $n{=}3$ seeds, while \emph{improving} (not regressing) ETTm2 ($-1.6\% \to -4.1\%$) and Weather ($-6.6\% \to -8.7\%$). The improvement is largest where non-stationarity is most pronounced, as expected.
77	2375:\emph{Per-dataset epoch mismatch (Batch~2).} A fixed $15$-epoch schedule assumes all datasets converge at the same rate. We test this by running validation-based early stopping with patience $5$ and logging the best epoch per seed. The result is striking: ETTh1 converges at epoch $7.2{\pm}1.2$, ETTh2 at $8.6{\pm}2.8$, ETTm1 at $14.8{\pm}3.7$, Weather at $8.6{\pm}1.2$, Electricity at $20.2{\pm}4.0$. No single fixed budget fits all six datasets---the $15$-epoch schedule simultaneously over-fits the ETTh pair and under-fits Electricity by $5$--$10$ epochs. Val-based early stopping (with gradient clipping at $\lVert g \rVert_2 \leq 1.0$ to stabilize the first epoch) achieves dataset-adaptive budgets without per-dataset hyperparameter tuning. In isolation this drops Electricity from $+0.6\%$ to $0.0\%$ (perfect parity, $p{=}0.991$) and Weather from $-6.6\%$ to $-4.6\%$ (nearing significance).
78	2377:\textbf{Phase~3 --- stacking validation.}
79	2381:\item \textbf{(iv)}~\emph{Shared raw branch} --- single $\text{Linear}(512, 96)$ shared across $K$ experts.
80	2382:\item \textbf{(v)}~\emph{NLinear raw architecture} --- $\mathbf{y} = \text{Linear}(\mathbf{x} - x_T) + x_T$.
81	2383:\item \textbf{(vi)}~\emph{Validation-based early stopping} --- patience $5$, gradient clipping $\lVert g \rVert_2 \leq 1.0$.

NLinear adds $\sim 3$ percentage points on ETTh2 ($0.365 \to 0.349$ when combined with shared). Val-early-stop contributes the Electricity perfect-parity result ($0.158 \to 0.156$) and the ETTm2 improvement ($0.197 \to 0.187$) by restoring an earlier checkpoint before the backbone residual begins overfitting. None of the three components alone achieves $5/6$; their combination does.

89	2566:\textbf{Architecture (E1, this work).} The Pure Raw-MLP MoE consists of $K{=}5$ size-diverse two-layer MLPs operating directly on the $512$-dimensional raw input window: hidden widths $\{64, 96, 128, 192, 256\}$ chosen so that the total parameter count ($449{,}557$) matches RR-MoA's $426$K within $5\%$. Each expert is $\text{Linear}(512{\to}h_k) \to \text{GELU} \to \text{Linear}(h_k{\to}96)$. The router is the same Conv1d $+$ AdaptiveAvgPool $+$ Linear architecture as RR-MoA, also operating on the raw input (not on hidden states). Top-2 sparse routing is used. Training protocol is identical to RR-MoA: Adam $1{\times}10^{-3}$, 15 epochs, batch 128. \textbf{No TSFM forward pass executes anywhere in this architecture} --- the $35$M-parameter MOMENT backbone is removed entirely.
90	2570:\caption{\textbf{Pure Raw-MLP MoE vs.\ Dual-Stream and DLinear} (5 seeds, mean$\pm$std, H$=$96). The Raw-MLP MoE has \emph{no TSFM at all}. It matches Dual-Stream within $\pm 7\%$ on every dataset, and \emph{beats} Dual-Stream outright on $\mathbf{3}$ of $\mathbf{6}$ datasets (ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity). Mean delta to Dual-Stream is $-1.5\%$ --- the TSFM's contribution is dataset-dependent and negligible in the average case.}
91	2584:\textbf{Mean}        & --- & --- & --- & $\mathbf{-1.5\%}$ & $\mathbf{+11.2\%}$ \\
92	2593:\textbf{What this does and does not say about the paper's core claims.} This finding does \emph{not} undermine the central diagnosis of the paper: normalization-induced routing collapse on hidden-state routers (AdaMix) is a real, mechanistically-explained phenomenon that affects every TSFM with internal RevIN, and pre-normalization routing (RR-MoA) is the minimal fix. That diagnosis is independent of whether the MOMENT backbone is the most efficient architecture for forecasting on every dataset --- it would remain true if the TSFM were replaced by any other backbone with internal instance normalization. What this finding \emph{does} say is that the dual-stream gap-closing story should be reframed: rather than ``the TSFM has complementary nonlinear features that dual-stream extracts,'' the more accurate reading is ``the TSFM contributes complementary signal on datasets with nonlinear dynamics (where it wins by $1$--$6\%$), and contributes essentially nothing on datasets where DLinear is already near-optimal (where pure raw-MLP MoE matches or beats it).''
93	2595:\textbf{Why we report this rather than hide it.} A pure raw-MLP MoE ablation was not in the original submission and was added in response to a reviewer who pointed out that, given Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}'s finding that the TSFM destroys nonlinear temporal structure, the obvious test of whether the TSFM is dead weight was missing. The reviewer's intuition was partially correct: the TSFM is dead weight on Weather, ETTm2, and Electricity, but not on the temperature-data datasets (ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1). We include the full result here because hiding it would invite the same attack at the next round of review, and because the dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an interesting empirical pattern that deserves attention from the TSFM-design community. The honest takeaway: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone --- it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.

55	\maketitle
56	
57	\begin{abstract}
58	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but using them on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. A natural improvement over today's single-head designs is a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head for each input window. We show that this natural design catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: the router collapses to a single expert and the remaining experts are never used. The cause, which we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, is that instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Standard MoE rescue mechanisms---load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, and expert-choice---do not recover performance, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of where collapse occurs, including boundary cases where it does not. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins on every dataset/freeze-level cell of the primary backbone, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} that we verify empirically: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse, so frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.
59	\end{abstract}
60	
61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter---more than the backbone choice---determines deployment utility.
65	
66	Today, every public TSFM benchmark uses a single static head that pools hidden states and projects them to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. Real time series are not so uniform: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical---one TSFM in GPU memory can serve thousands of tenants through small ($\sim$1.7\,MB) per-task adapters swapped in microseconds, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
67	
68	This natural mixture-of-experts approach catastrophically fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms---load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice---also do not help (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
69	
70	Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so frozen adapters should beat full fine-tuning, which we confirm empirically.
71	
72	\paragraph{Contributions.}
73	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, itemsep=0.15em, topsep=0.2em]
74	\item We identify \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, a failure mode of MoE adapters on TSFMs that is distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE-rescue literature, and isolate it through eight converging causal controls.
```

450	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). Two distinct mechanisms contribute. First, \emph{within MoE adapters}, unfreezing triggers gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared backbone, collapsing routing---freezing prevents this loop and preserves expert diversity. Second, \emph{across architectures}, the frozen five-expert mixture with learned per-sample routing provides an architectural advantage that a single unfrozen head cannot match even with full parameter access: per-sample expert selection captures input-regime structure that no single topology can represent. Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. A second architecture introduced below---\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA), in which each expert has its own sigmoid gate on raw input rather than a shared external router---independently replicates this pattern (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Even with careful hyperparameter tuning---learning rates $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-6}$, 100 epochs, cosine schedule with 10-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay, weight decay sweep, gradient accumulation up to effective batch 512 (90 configurations)---full fine-tuning still loses by $51$--$71\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
451	
452	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
```

514	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic (SR-MoA).} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, SR-MoA~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} replaces the external Conv1d router with a per-expert sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$, with no external router at all. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. The same SR-MoA architecture gating on hidden states degrades by $+87\%$ to $+768\%$ on \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($p{=}0.016$, Wilcoxon). \textbf{Normalization-specificity:} on Timer-XL (no RevIN), the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$---$15$--$100{\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT---confirming the degradation is normalization-induced. A third architecture-level control, \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) confirm the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight converging controls.
515	
516	\begin{table}[h]

512:\textbf{Three architectural instantiations of one principle.} The diagnosis says: restore raw-input access wherever normalization destroyed it. The paper proposes three variants, all confirming the same diagnosis from different angles. \emph{(i)}~\textbf{RR-MoA} (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:framework}, headline contribution): external Conv1d gate reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-K experts; experts read hidden states $\mathbf{H}$. \emph{(ii)}~\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}, architecture-agnostic validation): no external router---each expert has its own sigmoid gate on $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$. \emph{(iii)}~\textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}, diagnosis-actionability proof): each expert combines a shared NLinear raw-input branch with a gated backbone residual, which closes the DLinear gap.
557:\textbf{RR-MoA (Top-2, frozen)} & $\mathbf{0.680 \pm 0.027}$ & $\mathbf{0.564 \pm 0.059}$ & $\mathbf{0.276 \pm 0.017}$ & --- \\
559:\rowcolor{gray!8} DLinear (from scratch) & $0.416 \pm 0.002$ & $0.322 \pm 0.004$ & $0.208 \pm 0.003$ & --- \\
560:\rowcolor{gray!8} PatchTST/64$^\dagger$~\citep{nie2023patchtst} & $0.370$ & $0.274$ & $0.149$ & --- \\
561:\rowcolor{gray!8} iTransformer$^\ddagger$~\citep{liu2024itransformer} & $0.386$ & $0.334$ & $0.174$ & --- \\
1295:    % Box styles --- white-fill, thin colored strokes (academic convention)
1714:Dense $k{=}K{=}5$ & 5/5 & 100\% & $0.550 \pm 0.029$ & --- \\
2000: & \cellcolor{gray!8} DLinear (from scratch) & \cellcolor{gray!8} $0.149 \pm 0.017$ & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- & & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- \\
2005: & \cellcolor{gray!8} DLinear (from scratch) & \cellcolor{gray!8} $0.204 \pm 0.002$ & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- & & \cellcolor{gray!8} --- \\
2033:Backbone only & $44.3$ & $374$ & --- \\
2101:DLinear (scratch) & $\mathit{0.416}$ & $\mathit{0.322}$ & $\mathit{0.208}$ & --- \\
2328:\textbf{Average gap}          & --- & --- & $\mathbf{+1.6\%}$ & --- & \textbf{4/6 match-or-beat} \\
2367:\textbf{Phase~1 --- hypothesis falsification.}
2369:\emph{Shared raw branch succeeds.} At $K{=}5$ sharing a single $\text{Linear}(512, 96)$ module, raw parameters drop from $245$K to $49$K, exactly matching DLinear's budget. ETTh1 closes from $+5.1\%$ (sig loss) to $+1.6\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.086$); ETTh2 closes from $+10.6\%$ (sig loss) to $+7.0\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.078$). Per-sample gates and distinct backbone branches remain per-expert, so expert specialization is preserved in \emph{how} the backbone residual is invoked---only the DLinear-equivalent base predictor is shared. The co-occurrence of H1's failure and H2's success identifies parameter bloat, not data scarcity, as the root cause.
2373:\textbf{Phase~2 --- orthogonal refinements.}
2377:\textbf{Phase~3 --- stacking validation.}
2381:\item \textbf{(iv)}~\emph{Shared raw branch} --- single $\text{Linear}(512, 96)$ shared across $K$ experts.
2382:\item \textbf{(v)}~\emph{NLinear raw architecture} --- $\mathbf{y} = \text{Linear}(\mathbf{x} - x_T) + x_T$.
2383:\item \textbf{(vi)}~\emph{Validation-based early stopping} --- patience $5$, gradient clipping $\lVert g \rVert_2 \leq 1.0$.
2404:\textbf{Average gap} & --- & ($+1.6\%$) & --- & $\mathbf{-2.8\%}$ & --- & \textbf{5/6, 3 sig wins} \\
2476:Timer-XL    & $\mathbf{-10.4\%}$ & --- & $\mathbf{-16.8\%}$ & --- & $\mathbf{-27.9\%}$ & --- & $3/3$ \\

57	\begin{abstract}
58	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but using them on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. A natural improvement over today's single-head designs is a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head for each input window. We show that this natural design catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: the router collapses to a single expert and the remaining experts are never used. The cause, which we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, is that instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, and expert-choice) do not recover performance, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of where collapse occurs, including boundary cases where it does not. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins on every dataset/freeze-level cell of the primary backbone, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} that we verify empirically: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse, so frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.
59	\end{abstract}
```

  "new_string": "Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but downstream use requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. The natural improvement over today's single-head designs, a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head per input window, catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: routing entropy collapses to $0.000$ in $7/12$ unfrozen configurations. We call this \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. A $720$-run sweep across five standard rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) reduces MSE by at most $10.9\\%$, still $2.7\\times$ worse than our fix, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of when collapse matters (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$), correctly identifying boundary cases where raw routing does not help. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \\emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ dataset/freeze-level cells on the primary backbone with $27$--$77\\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \\emph{Frozen Paradox} we verify empirically: frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\\%$. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines."

> AGENT

Current title: **"Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters: Bypassing Routing Collapse in Normalized Time Series Foundation Models"**

Honest assessment: **decent but underwhelming.** Two issues:

1. **"Bypassing"** undersells the contribution. Your narrative is that the fix is the *minimal causal solution* derived from a diagnosis (mutual-information bound, signal-ratio predictor). "Bypassing" sounds like a workaround/hack, not a principled fix. Reviewers may read it as engineering rather than science.

2. **"Normalized Time Series Foundation Models"** is grammatically awkward — TSFMs aren't normalized; they *use* normalization internally. A skim-reader could parse it as "TSFMs that have been normalized."

3. The method-first framing buries the diagnosis. The strongest papers in this lineage (Grokking, Lottery Ticket, Mode Connectivity) lead with the *phenomenon* because the diagnosis is what reviewers remember and cite.

**Three alternatives worth considering:**

- **(diagnosis-first)** "Normalization-Induced Routing Collapse in Time Series Foundation Models"
- **(diagnosis + fix)** "When Normalization Breaks the Router: Diagnosing and Fixing Mixture-of-Expert Adapters on Time Series Foundation Models"
- **(method-first, cleaner)** "Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters: A Causal Fix for Routing Collapse on Foundation Models"

The first is most reviewer-magnetic (names a phenomenon they can cite). The second is most descriptive. The third keeps the method as the headline but replaces "Bypassing" with "Causal Fix" and broadens beyond TSFM-specific framing.

Want me to swap to one of these, or workshop further?

537	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why the simplicity of the fix is the point.} Identifying the correct cause is the hard part; that the fix follows trivially from the diagnosis is itself evidence the causal mechanism was correctly identified. The MoE community has long treated routing collapse as an \emph{optimization} problem (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss all attack the training dynamics), and these succeed when the router's input is informative but under-optimized. We show that normalization-induced collapse is a fundamentally different, \emph{information} problem: the routing signal is absent from the hidden states entirely, and none of the five rescue families (eleven configurations) we tested can compensate (720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).}

> AGENT

**Honest answer: only partially.** The paper has a defense paragraph (line 537, "Why the simplicity of the fix is the point") but the contribution bullet itself (line 76) reads as a benefits claim and does *not* pre-empt the supervisor's exact critique. A skim-reader sees "we restored raw access and closed the gap" and the critique lands cleanly.

The strongest counters to the "of course raw helps" view are scattered:
- 720-run rescue sweep (no raw access) recovers ≤10.9% — at line 537 and §4
- Naive re-injection (FiLM, SSR, $(\mu,\sigma)$) fails — at C8 of the causal-controls table and gap-closing section
- Signal-ratio predictor correctly identifies *boundary cases where raw routing does not help* — at contribution 2 (line 75) and Figure 2
- Negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL) — RR-MoA does not help when there is no instance norm

Those are all in the paper, but they're not aggregated into the contribution claim. The bullet should pre-empt the "trivially expected" framing.

**Proposed rewrite for line 76:**

> We propose RR-MoA, a minimal architectural fix that routes on raw pre-normalization input, and a companion variant (Residual-IA$^+$) that restores raw-signal access at the expert level. The non-triviality is not that raw access helps in the abstract, but *which* signal must be restored and *where*: naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection fails ($+42$–$239\%$ MSE; 11/18 cells re-collapse), standard rescue mechanisms that do not restore raw access fail ($720$ runs, $\leq 10.9\%$ recovery, $2.7\times$ worse than RR-MoA), and the signal-ratio predictor correctly flags datasets where raw routing should *not* help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). Together they close the gap to per-dataset supervised baselines while preserving frozen-backbone deployability.

This explicitly turns the supervisor's critique ("restoring raw access is trivially expected to help") into the *opposite* of the headline claim, by pointing at the four falsifying experiments. Want me to apply it?

 \maketitle
 
 \begin{abstract}
-Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but using them on a downstream task requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. A natural improvement over today's single-head designs is a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head for each input window. We show that this natural design catastrophically fails on TSFMs with instance normalization: the router collapses to a single expert and the remaining experts are never used. The cause, which we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}, is that instance normalization strips the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, and expert-choice) do not recover performance, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of where collapse occurs, including boundary cases where it does not. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins on every dataset/freeze-level cell of the primary backbone, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} that we verify empirically: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse, so frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.
+Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide powerful pretrained representations, but downstream use requires attaching a lightweight adapter head. The natural improvement over today's single-head designs, a mixture-of-expert (MoE) adapter that selects a different head per input window, catastrophically fails: routing entropy collapses to $0.000$ and the remaining experts are never used. We call this \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: the per-window mean and variance the router needs to distinguish input regimes are stripped by the instance normalization built into most pretrained TSFMs (MOMENT, TimesFM, and others). A $720$-run sweep across five standard rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) reduces MSE by at most $10.9\%$, still $2.7\times$ worse than our fix, because the failure is not in the router's optimization but in its input. We formalize the mechanism through a mutual-information decomposition that yields a signal-ratio predictor of when collapse matters (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$), correctly identifying boundary cases where raw routing does not help. The fix follows from the diagnosis: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ dataset/freeze-level cells on the primary backbone with $27$--$77\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter, generalizes across four further backbones, and is confirmed to be instance-normalization-specific by two negative controls. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox} we verify empirically: frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. A companion architecture (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes the remaining gap to supervised baselines.
 \end{abstract}

360	\end{figure}
361	\vspace{-1.2em}
362	
363	\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
364	
365	For a mixture of adapters to be effective, the expert pool must contain structurally diverse heads so that the router has meaningful choices. An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
366	
367	\textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Final evaluation uses the held-out test set.
368	
369	\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
370	\label{sec:rrmoa}
371	
372	The goal of per-window routing is to select, for each input window, the subset of expert adapters best suited to that window's characteristics, so that a quiet baseline and a volatile spike are handled by different adapter topologies rather than forced through the same head. As shown in the introduction, this fails when the router reads normalized hidden states, because RevIN removes the per-window statistics that distinguish input regimes. \textbf{RR-MoA} avoids this by splitting the information into two streams: the frozen TSFM produces deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$ for the experts, while a separate lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to decide which experts to activate:
373	\begin{equation}
374	\mathbf{Y} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} \underbrace{\widetilde{w}_j}_{\text{sparse weight}} \cdot \underbrace{\text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})}_{\text{deep embedding branch}},\quad
375	\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\right),\;\;
376	\widetilde{w} = \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})\right)
377	\label{eq:rrmoa}
378	\end{equation}
379	where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, and each $\text{Expert}_k$ is a canonical adapter head. Routing is restricted to the Top-$k$ experts with the highest gate logits, so only $k$ of $K$ experts execute per sample. With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
380	
381	\begin{algorithm}[h]
382	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
383	\label{alg:rrmoa}
384	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
385	\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
386	\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
387	\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
388	\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
389	\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
390	\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
391	\STATE \quad $\mathbf{o}_j\leftarrow \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Only the $k$ selected experts execute}
392	\STATE \textbf{end for}
393	\STATE $\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow \sum_{j\in\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{w}_j\cdot \mathbf{o}_j$
394	\RETURN $\mathbf{Y}$
395	\end{algorithmic}
396	\end{algorithm}
397	
398	\section{Experiments}
399	
400	\subsection{Setup}
401	
402	We evaluate RR-MoA on standard long-term forecasting benchmarks using five backbones, seven baselines, and up to five random seeds per configuration.
403	
404	\textbf{Backbones.} Primary: MOMENT-small~\citep{goswami2024moment} ($d{=}512$, 8 encoder blocks, internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}). Cross-backbone: MOMENT-large ($d{=}1024$, 24 blocks), Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, no RevIN), Moirai-MoE ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, sparse internal experts), Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} ($d{=}512$, 6 encoder blocks) and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} ($d{=}512$, 8 blocks, no RevIN; the latter two are negative controls; \S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Throughout, ``full fine-tuning'' for any backbone means \emph{all} encoder blocks are unfrozen (8/8 for MOMENT-small, 24/24 for MOMENT-large, 6/6 for Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos, 8/8 for Timer-XL). Primary results use a \emph{strictly frozen} backbone; freeze-level ablations in \S\ref{sec:freeze_ablation}.
405	
406	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Datasets and tasks.} ETTh1/h2/m1/m2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather (21ch), Electricity (321ch), standard LTSF benchmarks~\citep{godahewa2021monash}. Chronological splits, channel-wise StandardScaler normalization~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst}. We evaluate forecasting ($H{=}96$) and imputation (20\% masked). DLinear calibration: $0.416$/$0.322$/$0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather (Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}).}
407	
408	\textbf{Training.} Adam optimizer, lr$=10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Multi-seed experiments use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$) for the core table; ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
409

> AGENT

You're right — the section is mislabeled. Looking at line 363-367, it actually contains three different things:

1. **Adapter definition** $g_\phi$ — actual problem formulation (1 sentence)
2. **Expert pool spec** ($K{=}5$ canonical heads, AAS provenance, diversity ablation pointer) — design choice, not formulation
3. **"Standard protocol" paragraph** (line 367) — chronological splits, dataset sizes — this is *redundant* with §4 Setup (line 406 already says the same thing).

The section title "Problem Formulation and Expert Pool" is conjunctive precisely because the content was never really problem formulation. The expert-pool material is closer to a *design choice* of the method (which §3.2 RR-MoA introduces).

**Recommendation: collapse §3.1 entirely.**

- Move the $K{=}5$ expert pool spec into §3.2 RR-MoA, as a 1-2 sentence paragraph right after the algorithm or near where `Expert_k` is first referenced (line 379).
- Move the adapter definition $g_\phi$ into §3.2 opening (or drop it — the equation `Expert_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})` is self-defining).
- Delete the "Standard protocol" paragraph entirely (redundant with §4 Setup, line 406).

# Section/subsection naming audit vs NeurIPS 2025 conventions

## Main paper

| # | Current | Verdict | NeurIPS 2025 standard |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `\section{Method}` | **Acceptable, but bland.** | NeurIPS 2025 papers either keep "Method" or name the method (e.g., "Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters", "Approach", "The RR-MoA Framework"). With a named method, the latter is more memorable. |
| 2 | `\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}` | **Non-standard (conjunctive, mixed content).** | NeurIPS papers use either "Problem Setup" / "Preliminaries" *or* fold setup into the method. Conjunctive titles like "X and Y" are uncommon. |
| 3 | `\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}` | ✓ Good. | Named subsection matching the method. |
| 4 | `\subsection{Setup}` | ✓ Acceptable. | Often "Experimental Setup" but "Setup" is widely used. |
| 5 | `\subsection{RR-MoA: Routing Collapse and Its Resolution}` | **Non-standard (reads like a section, not subsection).** | The main-results subsection is almost always called "Main Results", "Results", or the topic e.g. "Routing Collapse on Instance-Normalized TSFMs". |
| 6 | `\section{Conclusion}` with a single sentence on limitations buried inside | **Risk: NeurIPS checklist asks explicitly about limitations.** | Standard is `\section{Conclusion and Limitations}` or a dedicated `\subsection{Limitations}` near the end. The checklist (you have one in `checklist.tex`) is graded against this. |

## Appendix

| Current | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `\section{AAS Search Details}` | **Jargon.** "AAS" is project-internal (Adapter Architecture Search). Should be expanded on first use. |
| `\subsection{Theoretical Depth of Observation X}` | **Unusual phrasing.** "Theoretical Depth" is not a standard formulation. Standard would be "Extended Analysis" or "Tightness of Observation X". |
| `\subsection{Residual-IA: Diagnosing and Fixing a Second Pathology}` | **Loaded language.** "Pathology" is an editorial choice that some reviewers flag as informal. Neutral: "Residual-IA: Closing the Remaining Gap" or "Residual-IA: Expert-Level Raw-Signal Restoration". |
| `\subsection{Pure Raw-MLP MoE Ablation: How Much Does the TSFM Actually Contribute?}` | **Question format is informal.** Most NeurIPS 2025 appendices use declarative titles. Better: "Pure Raw-MLP MoE Ablation: Quantifying the TSFM's Contribution". |
| `\section{Closing the DLinear Gap}` | ✓ Acceptable (DLinear is named in §4). |
| Other appendix sections | ✓ Mostly fine. |

## Highest-impact fixes (recommended priority order)

1. **Add `\subsection{Limitations}` to Conclusion.** NeurIPS 2025 reviewers almost always check this against the reproducibility checklist. Currently a single buried sentence — risk of checklist downgrade.
2. **Rename §4.2** from "RR-MoA: Routing Collapse and Its Resolution" → "Main Results" (or "Results").
3. **Drop or rename §3.1** (already discussed): rename to "Expert Pool" or fold into §3.2.
4. **Expand "AAS"** in the appendix to "Adapter Architecture Search Details" (first time it appears).
5. **Soften "Pathology"** → "Residual-IA: Closing the Remaining Gap".
6. **Convert question-title** in Pure Raw-MLP MoE appendix to declarative.

Lower-priority:
- Rename §3 "Method" → "Approach" or keep as is (purely stylistic).

Want me to apply (1)–(6) as a single pass?

Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. \\emph{(ii)~Signal-ratio boundary cases.} The signal-ratio predictor $R(\\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on Solar ($R{=}0.06$) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. \\emph{(iii)~Forecasting and imputation only.} We evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. 363	\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
364	
365	For a mixture of adapters to be effective, the expert pool must contain structurally diverse heads so that the router has meaningful choices. An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
366	
367	\textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Final evaluation uses the held-out test set.
368	
369	\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
370	\label{sec:rrmoa}
371	
372	The goal of per-window routing is to select, for each input window, the subset of expert adapters best suited to that window's characteristics, so that a quiet baseline and a volatile spike are handled by different adapter topologies rather than forced through the same head. As shown in the introduction, this fails when the router reads normalized hidden states, because RevIN removes the per-window statistics that distinguish input regimes. \textbf{RR-MoA} avoids this by splitting the information into two streams: the frozen TSFM produces deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$ for the experts, while a separate lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to decide which experts to activate:
373	\begin{equation}
374	\mathbf{Y} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} \underbrace{\widetilde{w}_j}_{\text{sparse weight}} \cdot \underbrace{\text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})}_{\text{deep embedding branch}},\quad
375	\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\right),\;\;
376	\widetilde{w} = \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})\right)
377	\label{eq:rrmoa}
378	\end{equation}
379	where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, and each $\text{Expert}_k$ is a canonical adapter head. Routing is restricted to the Top-$k$ experts with the highest gate logits, so only $k$ of $K$ experts execute per sample. With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
380	
381	\begin{algorithm}[h]
382	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
383	\label{alg:rrmoa}
384	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
```

395	
396	\subsection{Setup}
397	
398	We evaluate RR-MoA on standard long-term forecasting benchmarks using five backbones, seven baselines, and up to five random seeds per configuration.
399	
400	\textbf{Backbones.} Primary: MOMENT-small~\citep{goswami2024moment} ($d{=}512$, 8 encoder blocks, internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}). Cross-backbone: MOMENT-large ($d{=}1024$, 24 blocks), Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, no RevIN), Moirai-MoE ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, sparse internal experts), Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} ($d{=}512$, 6 encoder blocks) and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} ($d{=}512$, 8 blocks, no RevIN; the latter two are negative controls; \S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Throughout, ``full fine-tuning'' for any backbone means \emph{all} encoder blocks are unfrozen (8/8 for MOMENT-small, 24/24 for MOMENT-large, 6/6 for Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos, 8/8 for Timer-XL). Primary results use a \emph{strictly frozen} backbone; freeze-level ablations in \S\ref{sec:freeze_ablation}.
401	
402	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Datasets and tasks.} ETTh1/h2/m1/m2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather (21ch), Electricity (321ch), standard LTSF benchmarks~\citep{godahewa2021monash}. Chronological splits, channel-wise StandardScaler normalization~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst}. We evaluate forecasting ($H{=}96$) and imputation (20\% masked). DLinear calibration: $0.416$/$0.322$/$0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather (Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}).}
403	
404	\textbf{Training.} Adam optimizer, lr$=10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Multi-seed experiments use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$) for the core table; ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
405	
406	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Baselines.} Frozen: (i)~three fixed adapters (linear, attention, conv), (ii)~LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), (iii)~TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, (iv)~independent ensemble (5 experts, no router), (v)~AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} (hidden-state MoE). Unfrozen: (vi)~full fine-tuning (all 8 blocks, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs). Calibration anchor: (vii)~DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} (from scratch).}
407	
408	\textbf{Reproducibility.} All code, raw JSON results, per-seed routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data are included in the supplementary material and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
409	
410	\subsection{Main Results}
411	\label{sec:main_results}
412	\label{sec:architectures}
413	\label{sec:freeze_ablation}
414	
415	RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against a comprehensive baseline suite.
416	
417	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{RR-MoA vs.\ fixed adapters.} Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} compares Top-2 RR-MoA against the best fixed adapter, using the \emph{same five expert heads} in all rows. Across 6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ \textbf{54 configurations}, RR-MoA wins every one with tight standard deviations.}
418	
419	\begin{table}[h]
```

Main paper §4.1 went from ~290 words → ~165 words (5 paragraphs → 4 tighter paragraphs):

**New `\section{Experimental Setup Details}` appendix** (right after `\appendix`) holds the deferred detail: backbone specs, full-FT definition, training hyperparameters, seed values, per-baseline configurations with cross-references to the existing baseline-specific appendices.

1873:\caption{\textbf{DLinear supervised calibration} (trained from scratch, no backbone, 3 seeds, test MSE mean$\pm$std). The gap to RR-MoA is expected; the frozen-backbone paradigm is motivated by deployment constraints (\S\ref{sec:intro}).}
2054:The RR-MoA routing overhead ($+4.3$\,ms) is dominated by the sparse expert selection loop; the router itself (Conv1d + linear, ${\sim}1.1$K params) adds $<0.1$\,ms. At deployment scale, adapter swapping between tenants requires loading $426$K parameters (${\sim}1.7$\,MB) from host RAM, orders of magnitude cheaper than reloading a $35$M parameter backbone. At $N{=}100$ tenants, adapter-swapping throughput is $141$ tenants/sec. The memory footprint of a shared-backbone deployment ($1\times$ TSFM at $140$\,MB plus $100\times$ RR-MoA adapters at $1.7$\,MB each $= 310$\,MB total) is $45\times$ smaller than naively replicating the full backbone per tenant ($100 \times 140$\,MB $= 14\,000$\,MB). \textbf{Honest caveat:} this $45\times$ figure is against the naive per-tenant-TSFM baseline, which no rational deployer would run; against per-tenant DLinear ($100 \times 49$K parameters $= 19.6$\,MB total) the shared-TSFM approach is roughly $15\times$ \emph{larger}, not smaller. The memory argument for TSFMs over DLinear is therefore not the core motivation; that role is played by cross-task reuse, zero-shot onboarding, and the elimination of $O(N)$ per-tenant training pipelines (see \S\ref{sec:intro} and Appendix~\ref{app:deployment}).

659	\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
660	\vspace{-0.4em}
661	
662	MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail for structural reasons: the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics a downstream router needs. Raw pre-normalization routing fixes the failure and motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}; $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies.
663	
664	\paragraph{Limitations.} Three boundaries scope our claims. \emph{(i)~Normalization specificity.} Our diagnosis applies to backbones with instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm); backbones without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain, as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts. Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. \emph{(ii)~Signal-ratio boundary cases.} The signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on Solar ($R{=}0.06$) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. \emph{(iii)~Forecasting and imputation only.} We evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. Anomaly detection, classification, and irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred to future work.
665	
666	\label{page:end_main}
667	\bibliographystyle{plainnat}
668	\begin{thebibliography}{20}
```

**Honest answer: yes, slightly thin in the body, but well-served by the Limitations.**

Current shape (line 659-664):
- **Body recap**: 1 sentence + 1 sentence (~50 words). It's basically a restatement of the abstract, which doesn't add new perspective.
- **Limitations**: 3 well-scoped boundaries (~140 words). This part is good.

What standard NeurIPS conclusions add beyond a recap:

1. **Broader implication** — "this matters beyond TSFMs because…"
2. **Insight / takeaway** — what the field should learn
3. **Future work** — concrete next steps

Your body has the recap but skips (1)–(3). A reviewer scanning the conclusion may walk away thinking "OK but so what's the lesson?"

**Proposed minimal addition (~70 words, 1 short paragraph)** that would land between the body and Limitations:

> \textbf{Implication.} Routing collapse on instance-normalized backbones generalizes a broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal that a downstream router needs, optimization-side rescues (load balancing, z-loss, ReLU routing) cannot recover what is structurally absent. The fix is to restore the missing input, not to retune the optimizer. This perspective generalizes beyond time series; the same mechanism arises in vision (Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}) and predicts where future MoE-on-FM systems will and will not benefit from raw-signal access.

This adds the broader-implication paragraph the conclusion currently lacks while staying tight. **However**, your CLAUDE.md notes the paper is "page 9 compliant" — so a 70-word addition could push it over. I'd recommend:

1. First check current page break (where `\label{page:end_main}` lands).
2. If there's slack on page 9: add the implication paragraph.
3. If page 9 is already at the boundary: skip it; current conclusion is acceptable.

61:\section{Introduction}
72:\paragraph{Contributions.}
79:\begin{figure}[t]
220:\caption{\textbf{Problem overview.} \textbf{(a)}~Today's TSFM benchmarks adapt the frozen backbone with a \emph{single static head} that pools hidden states for every input window, regardless of the window's per-sample statistics. \textbf{(b)}~The natural extension, a mixture of expert adapters with a learned router, catastrophically fails on instance-normalized TSFMs: routing collapses to a single expert (entropy $0.000{\pm}0.000$), and the remaining experts receive no gradient. Both panels share the same backbone; only the head changes.}
225:\section{Related Work}
237:\section{Method}
240:\begin{figure}[t!]
357:\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A} (blue): the frozen TSFM produces hidden states $\mathbf{H}$ (no gradients through $\theta$); all experts receive $\mathbf{H}$. \textit{Path~B} (orange): a lightweight Conv1d gate (1{,}100 params) reads the raw pre-normalization signal $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects the Top-2 experts via sparse routing. Only the selected experts (bold) contribute to the output; inactive experts (gray) receive no gradient. Standard MoE routing on $\mathbf{H}$ collapses (entropy $0.000{\pm}0.000$; Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
363:\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
378:\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}

396:\subsection{Setup}
406:\subsection{Main Results}

417:\caption{\textbf{Freeze-level ablation} (test MSE, mean$\pm$std, 3 seeds, H=96). RR-MoA (Top-2, same 5 experts) vs.\ best of $\{$linear, attention, conv$\}$. RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} across 6 datasets (extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}); improvement is largest when frozen (``Frozen Paradox'').}
451:\begin{table}[h]
453:\caption{\textbf{AdaMix routing collapse} (entropy max ${\approx}1.609$). Under unfreezing, entropy ${\to}0.000$. \colorbox{green!8}{Green}: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy ($0.66$--$1.32$), proving RevIN is the causal mechanism. Extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}
483:\begin{figure}[h]
487:\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}50$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~Dominant expert's gradient grows while others starve (co-adaptation loop, \S\ref{sec:rrmoa}). Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) shows the trajectory inverts.}
492:\begin{figure}[h]
496:\caption{\textbf{Causal contrast: MOMENT vs Timer-XL} (ETTh1, last-4 unfreezing). Pattern generalizes across all 3 tested datasets.}
508:\begin{table}[h]

Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. \emph{(ii)~Signal-ratio boundary cases.} The signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on Solar ($R{=}0.06$) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. \emph{(iii)~Forecasting and imputation only.} We evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. 375	\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
376	
377	\begin{algorithm}[h]
378	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
379	\label{alg:rrmoa}
380	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
381	\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
382	\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
383	\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
384	\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
385	\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
386	\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
387	\STATE \quad $\mathbf{o}_j\leftarrow \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Only the $k$ selected experts execute}
388	\STATE \textbf{end for}
389	\STATE $\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow \sum_{j\in\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{w}_j\cdot \mathbf{o}_j$
390	\RETURN $\mathbf{Y}$
391	\end{algorithmic}
392	\end{algorithm}
393	
394	\section{Experiments}
395	
396	\subsection{Setup}
