Subsequently, we will do linear probing and (then) finetuning of the pretrained model on the`train` part of the target data, and will validate the forecasting performance on the`test` part of the target data.\n\nThe`PatchTST` model was proposed in A Time Series is Worth 64 Words: Long-term Forecasting with Transformers by Yuqi Nie, Nam H. Nguyen, Phanwadee Sinthong, Jayant Kalagnanam and presented at ICLR 2023.\n\n## Quick overview of PatchTST\n\nAt a high level, the model vectorizes individual time series in a batch into patches of a given size and encodes the resulting sequence of vectors via a Transformer that then outputs the prediction length forecast via an appropriate head.\n\nThe model is based on two key components:\n\n1. segmentation of time series into subseries-level patches which serve as input tokens to the Transformer;\n2. channel-independence where each channel contains a single univariate time series that shares the same embedding and Transformer weights across all the series, i.e. a global univariate model.\n\nThe patching design naturally has three-fold benefit:\n\n- local semantic information is retained in the embedding;\n- computation and memory usage of the attention maps are quadratically reduced given the same look-back window via strides between patches; and\n- the model can attend longer history via a trade-off between the patch length (input vector size) and the context length (number of sequences).\n\nIn addition, PatchTST has a modular design to seamlessly support masked time series pre-training as well as direct time series forecasting.\n\n| (a) PatchTST model overview where a batch of MM time series each of length LL are processed independently (by reshaping them into the batch dimension) via a Transformer backbone and then reshaping the resulting batch back into MM series of prediction length TT. Transformer-based models have emerged as a powerful approach, but their performance depends critically on the representation of temporal data. Traditional point-wise representations preserve individual time-step information, enabling fine-grained modeling, yet they tend to be computationally expensive and less effective at modeling broader contextual dependencies, limiting their scalability to long sequences. Patch-wise representations aggregate consecutive steps into compact tokens to improve efficiency and model local temporal dynamics, but they often discard fine-grained temporal details that are critical for accurate predictions in volatile or complex time series. We propose IPatch, a multi-resolution Transformer architecture that integrates both point-wise and patch-wise tokens, modeling temporal information at multiple resolutions. Experiments on 7 benchmark datasets demonstrate that IPatch consistently improves forecasting accuracy, robustness to noise, and generalization across various prediction horizons compared to single-representation baselines.\n\n###### keywords:\n\nTime series forecasting , Data Representation , Transformers , Patching , Autocorrelation\n\n††journal: Information Fusion\n\n\\affiliation\n\n[a]organization=CCPS Laboratory, ENSAM, Hassan II University, city=Casablanca, postcode=20670, country=Morocco\n\n\\affiliation\n\n[b]organization=IRIT, UMR 5505 CNRS, Université Toulouse Capitole, city=Toulouse, postcode=31000, country=France\n\n\\affiliation\n\n[c]organization=IRIT, UMR 5505 CNRS, Université Toulouse, UT2J, city=Toulouse, postcode=31000, country=France\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nTime-series forecasting has increasingly adopted Transformer architectures, whose self-attention mechanism enables the modeling of long-range and heterogeneous temporal dependencies [23]. A central design aspect in adapting these models to temporal data is the choice of input representation (point-wise[33] or patch-wise[17]). Traditional point-wise encodings, in which each time step is treated as an individual token, preserve full temporal resolution and allow the model to capture fine-grained local variations [2]. This property is particularly important in settings characterized by volatility or rapid fluctuations. However, point-wise representations incur substantial computational costs for long input sequences and offer limited capacity to capture broader temporal structure in the self-attention mechanism [30]. The proposed patch is as follows.\n\n-torch.randn((self.batch_size, 3, 224, 224)).to(self.device)\n+torch.randn((self.batch_size, 3, 224, 224), device=self.device)"

```
      "text": "Where Do the Joules Go? Diagnosing Inference Energy Consumption\nJae-Won Chung 1 Ruofan Wu 1 Jeff J. Ma 1 Mosharaf Chowdhury 1\nAbstract\nEnergy is now a critical ML computing resource.\nWhile measuring energy consumption and observ\u0002ing trends is a valuable first step, accurately un\u0002derstanding and diagnosing why those differences\noccur is crucial for optimization. To that end, we\nbegin by presenting a large-scale measurement\nstudy of inference time and energy across the gen\u0002erative AI landscape with 46 models, 7 tasks, and\n1,858 different configurations on NVIDIA H100\nand B200 GPUs. Our empirical findings span\norder-of-magnitude variations: LLM task type\ncan lead to 25× energy differences, video gener\u0002ation sometimes consumes more than 100× the\nenergy of images, and GPU utilization differences\ncan result in 3–5× energy differences. Based on\nour observations, we present a framework for rea\u0002soning about the underlying mechanisms that gov\u0002ern time and energy consumption. The essence is\nthat time and energy are determined by latent met\u0002rics like memory and utilization, which are in turn\naffected by various factors across the algorithm,\nsoftware, and hardware layers. Our framework\nalso extends directly to throughput per watt, a\ncritical metric for power-constrained datacenters.\n1. Introduction\nAs AI infrastructure scales to meet rapid growth in AI com\u0002pute demand, energy consumption is becoming a critical\nbottleneck (CBRE, 2023; 2024; 2025; McKinsey & Com\u0002pany, 2023; 2024; 2025; Moss, 2024; International Energy\nAgency, 2025; Kou, 2025). This is primarily due to a mis\u0002match between energy demand and supply—building new\npower sources to supply energy takes years of planning, ap\u0002proval, and construction (US EIA, 2024; Chen et al., 2025),\nwhereas AI compute demand skyrockets relentlessly.\nYet, energy has not been a mainstream metric for the ML\ncommunity, which has led to a lack of tools to measure and\nunderstand energy consumption. Recently, works like the\n1University of Michigan & The ML.ENERGY Initiative. Cor\u0002respondence to: Jae-Won Chung.\nPreprint. February 2, 2026.\nML.ENERGY Benchmark (Chung et al., 2025) helped fill\nthis gap with tools that provide energy measurements in\nenvironments that are representative of real-world deploy\u0002ments. However, while measuring energy is necessary, it is\nnot sufficient; without understanding the underlying mech\u0002anisms that govern the measured end metrics, we cannot\nreason about why one configuration consumes more time\nor energy than another, nor can we guide ourselves through\nthe optimization space.\nThe goal of this paper is to provide a framework for under\u0002standing and reasoning about inference energy consumption\nin generative AI workloads.       "text": "arXiv preprint\nUNDERSTANDING EFFICIENCY: QUANTIZATION,\nBATCHING, AND SERVING STRATEGIES IN LLM\nENERGY USE\nJulien Delavande\nHugging Face\nENS Paris-Saclay\njulien.delavande@ens-paris-saclay.fr\nRegis Pierrard & Sasha Luccioni\nHugging Face\n{regis,sasha.luccioni}\n@huggingface.co\nABSTRACT\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in production, contribut\u0002ing towards shifting the burden in terms of computational resources and energy\ndemands from training to inference. While prior work has examined the energy\ncost of inference per prompt or per token, we highlight how system-level design\nchoices - such as numerical precision, batching strategy, and request scheduling -\ncan lead to orders-of-magnitude differences in energy consumption for the same\nmodel. We perform a detailed empirical study of LLM inference energy and la\u0002tency on NVIDIA H100 GPUs, analyzing the impact of quantization, batch size,\nand serving configuration (e.g., with Hugging Face’s Text Generation Inference\nserver). Our results reveal that lower-precision formats only yield energy gains in\ncompute-bound regimes; that batching improves energy efficiency, especially in\nmemory-bound phases like decoding; and that structured request timing (arrival\nshaping) can reduce per-request energy by up to 100×. We argue that sustainable\nLLM deployment depends not only on model internals, but also on the orchestra\u0002tion of the serving stack. Our findings motivate phase-aware energy profiling and\nsystem-level optimizations for greener AI services.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nAs large language models (LLMs) transition from research prototypes to real-world services, the\nenergy consumed during inference has become a growing concern (Wu et al., 2021; Luccioni et al.,\n2024b; Everman et al., 2023). While training once dominated the environmental cost of AI (Strubell\net al., 2019; Patterson et al., 2021), the widespread deployment of LLMs in user-facing applications -\nchatbots, assistants, code generators - has shifted the spotlight toward deployment efficiency. Studies\nhave called for improved energy reporting (Henderson et al., 2020; Luccioni et al., 2024b), quantified\nthe energy per query (Samsi et al., 2023), and highlighted the role of verbosity (Poddar et al., 2025;\nGao et al., 2024; Jin et al., 2025) and prompt length (Wilkins et al., 2024a) in power consumption.\nHowever, efficiency remains underexplored in deployment conditions (Fernandez et al., 2025).\nIn this paper, we explore these systemic factors in detail.       "text": "Inference economics of language models | Epoch AI\n\n## Introduction\n\nAs the capabilities of AI models have expanded, and as the recent paradigm of test-time compute scaling has taken off, the demand for AI inference has grown enormously. Inference revenue at major AI companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic has been growing at a rate of 3x per year or more, even as their models continue to become smaller and cheaper compared to 2023.\n\nA few years ago, the benchmark for whether a language model was fast enough was “human reading speed”: if a model could generate 10 tokens per second when responding to a user, that was good enough. Now, as models are asked to reason at length about complex problems and are placed inside elaborate agentic loops, this benchmark has become obsolete. The benefits to serving models faster for inference are greater than ever before. Despite this, there has been little work investigating how language models can be served quickly at scale and how much we can increase their speed at the expense of paying a higher price per token.\n\nToday, we’re releasing a model of LLM inference economics which helps answer these questions. Working with the model reveals many important facts about inference at scale that are not widely appreciated.\n\n## How does the model work?\n\nThe basic idea behind the model is to decompose the time taken during the forward pass of a Transformer into four components:\n\n- Arithmetic time, which is the time taken by the cores of a GPU to perform the actual addition and multiplication operations.\n- Memory read-write time, which is the time needed to load information from high-bandwidth memory (HBM) into the cores. A more detailed approach would model the entire memory hierarchy instead of just HBM, but we don’t do this.\n- Network send-receive time, which is computed by dividing the amount of information each GPU receives by its receive-only network bandwidth.\n- Latency, which is the fixed time taken up by operations such as kernel launches and GPU collectives independent of their size.       "text": "Achieve 23x LLM Inference Throughput & Reduce p50 Latency\n\nHome Blog Blog Detail\n\n# How continuous batching enables 23x throughput in LLM inference while reducing p50 latency\n\nBy Cade Daniel, Chen Shen, Eric Liang and Richard Liaw| June 22, 2023\n\nIn this blog, we’ll cover the basics of large language model (LLM) inference and highlight inefficiencies in traditional batching policies. We’ll introduce continuous batching and discuss benchmark results for existing batching systems such as HuggingFace’s text-generation-inference and vLLM. By leveraging vLLM, users can achieve 23x LLM inference throughput while reducing p50 latency.Due to the large GPU memory footprint and compute cost of LLMs, serving dominates the compute cost for most real world applications. ML engineers often treat LLMs like \"black boxes\" that can only be optimized with internal changes such as quantization and custom CUDA kernels. However, this is not entirely the case. Because LLMs iteratively generate their output, and because LLM inference is often memory and not compute bound, there are surprising system-level batching optimizations that make 10x or more differences in real-world workloads.\n\nOne recent such proposed optimization is continuous batching, also known as dynamic batching, or batching with iteration-level scheduling. We wanted to see how this optimization performs. However, at a high level, LLM inference is pretty straightforward.\n\nFor each request:\n\nYou start with a sequence of tokens (called the \"prefix\" or \"prompt\").\n\nThe LLM produces a sequence of completion tokens, stopping only after producing a stop token or reaching a maximum sequence length.\n\nThis is an iterative process. Gary Chan1\n1HKUST, 2Rutgers University, 3Texas State University\nAbstract\nTo design fast neural networks, many works have been\nfocusing on reducing the number of floating-point opera\u0002tions (FLOPs). Alvarez,\nJan Kautz, Pavlo Molchanov\nNVIDIA\n{ahatamizadeh, gheinrich, dannyy, atao, josea, jkautz, pmolchanov}@nvidia.com\nAbstract\nWe design a new family of hybrid CNN-ViT neural net\u0002works, named FasterViT, with a focus on high image through\u0002put for computer vision (CV) applications. Our newly\nintroduced Hierarchical Attention (HAT) approach decom\u0002poses global self-attention with quadratic complexity into\na multi-level attention with reduced computational costs.\nWe benefit from efficient window-based self-attention.       "id": "https://arxiv.org/html/2507.18537v2",
```
```
      "title": "TTS-VAR: A Test-Time Scaling Framework for Visual Auto-Regressive Generation",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2507.18537v2",
      "text": "# TTS-VAR: A Test-Time Scaling Framework for Visual Auto-Regressive Generation\n\nZhekai Chen1 Ruihang Chu2∗ Yukang Chen3 Shiwei Zhang2 Yujie Wei2 Yingya Zhang2 Xihui Liu1∗ 1 HKU MMLab 2 Tongyi Lab, Alibaba Group 3 CUHK zkchen66@outlook.com\n\n###### Abstract\n\nScaling visual generation models is essential for real-world content creation, yet requires substantial training and computational expenses. Alternatively, test-time scaling has garnered growing attention due to resource efficiency and promising performance. In this work, we present TTS-VAR, the first general test-time scaling framework for visual auto-regressive (VAR) models, modeling the generation process as a path searching problem. To dynamically balance computational efficiency with exploration capacity, we first introduce an adaptive descending batch size schedule throughout the causal generation process. Besides, inspired by VAR’s hierarchical coarse-to-fine multi-scale generation, our framework integrates two key components: (i) At coarse scales, we observe that generated tokens are hard for evaluation, possibly leading to erroneous acceptance of inferior samples or rejection of superior samples. Noticing that the coarse scales contain sufficient structural information, we propose clustering-based diversity search. It preserves structural variety through semantic feature clustering, enabling later selection on samples with higher potential. (ii) In fine scales, resampling-based potential selection prioritizes promising candidates using potential scores, which are defined as reward functions incorporating multi-scale generation history. Experiments on the powerful VAR model Infinity2B show a notable 8.7% GenEval score improvement (0.69→0.75). Key insights reveal that early-stage structural features effectively influence final quality, and resampling efficacy varies across generation scales. Code is available at https://github.com/ali-vilab/TTS-VAR.\n\n††* Corresponding Authors\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nRecent years have witnessed significant progress in image generative models [1, 2, 3, 4]. Previous text-to-image generative models primarily rely on diffusion models [5, 6], which iteratively denoise the latent to generate high-quality images from random noise. Yet, advancements in large language models (LLMs) [7, 8, 9] have spurred interest in Auto-Regressive (AR) architectures for image generation [10, 11, 12], leveraging sequential modeling to capture visual patterns. Among these, Visual Auto-Regressive Modeling (VAR) [13, 14] has emerged as a groundbreaking paradigm. **Verified: standard practice for non-autoregressive forward-pass models.** Found supporting examples + identified the one case where it would NOT be appropriate (which is not ours).
```

**Standard practice (synthetic random tensors) — used routinely in:**
- **FasterNet** (CVPR 2023, "Run, Don't Walk: Chasing Higher FLOPS"): latency on dummy `(B, 3, H, W)` tensors at standard ImageNet shapes
- **EfficientDet** (Tan et al., CVPR 2020): FLOPs/latency on synthetic shapes
- **FasterViT** (NVIDIA): image throughput on dummy inputs
- **MDP** (CVPR 2025): pruning with latency constraint, dummy tensor benchmarks
- **`pytorch/benchmark` (TorchBench)**: official PyTorch benchmark suite uses `torch.randn` for vision model latency
- **"Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference"** (Pope et al., MLSys 2023): synthetic inputs for fixed-shape forward latency

**The one case where synthetic inputs are flagged as misleading** — and why it doesn't apply to us:

**SPEED-Bench (NVIDIA, NeurIPS-area 2026)**: explicitly notes "synthetic inputs *overestimate* real-world throughput" — but specifically for **LLM autoregressive decoding** where output length depends on input content (heterogeneous decode lengths cause batch underutilization). This is a *throughput*-vs-*latency* concern in *autoregressive* generation. Our setting is non-autoregressive: fixed input window of $L=512$, fixed forecast horizon $H=96$, single deterministic forward pass per batch. The data path is data-oblivious — no early exit, no content-dependent sparsity, no token-by-token generation.

**Conclusion**: synthetic random inputs at the canonical LTSF shape `(B=128, L=512)` are the correct methodology for our table. The numbers stand.

**Small reviewer-defensive edit worth making**: append a one-liner to the `tab:benchmark` caption noting "synthetic random inputs at the standard LTSF batch shape (B=128, L=512); for non-autoregressive deterministic forward passes such as TSFM forecasting, latency is data-oblivious." This pre-empts a reviewer who reads SPEED-Bench and asks the question.

Want me to add that one-liner to the caption, then commit and push the whole batch?

```
68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach catastrophically fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) also do not help (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
229:\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes are complementary rather than competing: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
231:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse is a well-known failure mode addressed by load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}; AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \emph{input} carries discriminative information. Sweeping the five families across eleven configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports this view: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
357:\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A} (blue): frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$ for all experts. \textit{Path~B} (orange): Conv1d gate (1{,}100 params) reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-2 experts; inactive experts (gray) receive no gradient. Standard hidden-state MoE collapses (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
396:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{RR-MoA vs.\ fixed adapters.} Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} compares Top-2 RR-MoA against the best fixed adapter, using the \emph{same five expert heads} in all rows. Across 6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ \textbf{54 configurations}, RR-MoA wins every one with tight standard deviations.}
425:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). Two distinct mechanisms contribute. First, \emph{within MoE adapters}, unfreezing triggers gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared backbone, collapsing routing, and freezing prevents this loop and preserves expert diversity. Second, \emph{across architectures}, the frozen five-expert mixture with learned per-sample routing provides an architectural advantage that a single unfrozen head cannot match even with full parameter access: per-sample expert selection captures input-regime structure that no single topology can represent. Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. A second architecture introduced below, \textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA), in which each expert has its own sigmoid gate on raw input rather than a shared external router, independently replicates this pattern (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Even with careful hyperparameter tuning (learning rates $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-6}$, 100 epochs, cosine schedule with 10-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay, weight decay sweep, gradient accumulation up to effective batch 512, 90 configurations), full fine-tuning still loses by $51$--$71\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
432:{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms it in the full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Independent ensembles are $37$--$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains.}
464:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix}, which uses the standard recipe of routing on hidden states. Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}
470:\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}50$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~Dominant expert's gradient grows while others starve (co-adaptation loop, \S\ref{sec:rrmoa}). Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) shows the trajectory inverts.}
483:In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $27\%$ to $77\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
485:\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{zhang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven configurations on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) is catastrophic ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
487:\textbf{Three architectural instantiations of one principle.} The diagnosis says: restore raw-input access wherever normalization destroyed it. The paper proposes three variants, all confirming the same diagnosis from different angles. \emph{(i)}~\textbf{RR-MoA} (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:framework}, headline contribution): external Conv1d gate reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-K experts; experts read hidden states $\mathbf{H}$. \emph{(ii)}~\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}, architecture-agnostic validation): no external router, each expert has its own sigmoid gate on $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$. \emph{(iii)}~\textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}, diagnosis-actionability proof): each expert combines a shared NLinear raw-input branch with a gated backbone residual, which closes the DLinear gap.
489:\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic (SR-MoA).} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, SR-MoA~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} replaces the external Conv1d router with a per-expert sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$, with no external router at all. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. The same SR-MoA architecture gating on hidden states degrades by $+87\%$ to $+768\%$ on \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($p{=}0.016$, Wilcoxon). \textbf{Normalization-specificity:} on Timer-XL (no RevIN), the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), confirming the degradation is normalization-induced. A third architecture-level control, \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) confirm the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight converging controls.
500:C1 & RevIN ablation & Is RevIN the cause? & Entropy $0.000 \to 0.66$--$1.32$ (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}) \\
501:C2 & MOMENT vs Timer-XL & Same code, only norm differs & Opposite trajectories (Fig.~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) \\
503:C4 & Rescue sweep (5 families) & Optimization problem? & 0/11 configs recover; $2.7{\times}$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}) \\
505:C6 & SR-MoA raw vs hidden & Generalizes beyond RR-MoA? & Hidden: $+87$--$768\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}) \\
512:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why the simplicity of the fix is the point.} Identifying the correct cause is the hard part; that the fix follows trivially from the diagnosis is itself evidence the causal mechanism was correctly identified. The MoE community has long treated routing collapse as an \emph{optimization} problem (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss all attack the training dynamics), and these succeed when the router's input is informative but under-optimized. We show that normalization-induced collapse is a fundamentally different, \emph{information} problem: the routing signal is absent from the hidden states entirely, and none of the five rescue families (eleven configurations) we tested can compensate (720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).}
544:\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). As the diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes this gap: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert's output; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) matches or beats DLinear on \textbf{6/6 datasets} at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$), generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones.\label{tab:gap_closing_headline}
546:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}, Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), confirming the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\,\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a \emph{phase transition} (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs): MSE degrades mildly for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ ($+5.4\%$) but catastrophically at $\alpha{=}1.0$ ($+283\%$), while entropy stays in $[1.43, 1.57]$: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth}). A temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
564:In the limit where the router's signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$), the bound saturates and $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We therefore use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives $\varepsilon \in [0.09, 1.26]$ nats, cross-validated within ${\pm}0.26$ nats by KSG~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; a held-out classifier shows normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging $0.075$ nats (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}). Training $\alpha \in [0,1]$ as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to $\alpha < 0.5$ on all six datasets ($30/30$ runs; Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}); $H(E \mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$), since routing \emph{potential} in the stripped statistics matters, not the MI loss itself. Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly predicted as a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR ablation $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}). Full proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.
639:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task and horizon generalization.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins all 21 MOMENT-small/large configurations ($27$--$81\%$; Table~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}), 18/18 Moirai ($3$--$31\%$), and 6/6 Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} datasets ($37$--$90\%$; Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}): adapter-level and backbone-internal routing operate on complementary axes. The negative controls predicted by Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} (Chronos and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, neither using instance normalization) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain (4/18 within chance; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}). Top-2 sparse routing retains most of the dense gain at a $+10\%$ latency cost (Appendix~\ref{app:topk}); imputation ($7/8$; Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}) and horizons $H{\in}\{96{,}192{,}336{,}720\}$ (24/24; Table~\ref{tab:horizon}) confirm robustness. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) extends to all 6 datasets across 5 backbones and 4 horizons (Table~\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_mhcb}).}
---ORPHAN LABEL CHECK (gap_closing_headline)---
425:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). Two distinct mechanisms contribute. First, \emph{within MoE adapters}, unfreezing triggers gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared backbone, collapsing routing, and freezing prevents this loop and preserves expert diversity. Second, \emph{across architectures}, the frozen five-expert mixture with learned per-sample routing provides an architectural advantage that a single unfrozen head cannot match even with full parameter access: per-sample expert selection captures input-regime structure that no single topology can represent. Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. A second architecture introduced below, \textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA), in which each expert has its own sigmoid gate on raw input rather than a shared external router, independently replicates this pattern (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Even with careful hyperparameter tuning (learning rates $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-6}$, 100 epochs, cosine schedule with 10-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay, weight decay sweep, gradient accumulation up to effective batch 512, 90 configurations), full fine-tuning still loses by $51$--$71\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
483:In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $27\%$ to $77\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
544:\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). As the diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes this gap: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert's output; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) matches or beats DLinear on \textbf{6/6 datasets} at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$), generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones.\label{tab:gap_closing_headline}
564:In the limit where the router's signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$), the bound saturates and $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We therefore use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives $\varepsilon \in [0.09, 1.26]$ nats, cross-validated within ${\pm}0.26$ nats by KSG~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; a held-out classifier shows normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging $0.075$ nats (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}). Training $\alpha \in [0,1]$ as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to $\alpha < 0.5$ on all six datasets ($30/30$ runs; Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}); $H(E \mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$), since routing \emph{potential} in the stripped statistics matters, not the MI loss itself. Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly predicted as a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR ablation $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}). Full proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.
634:\label{fig:frozen_paradox}
635:\label{fig:signal_ratio}
1097:Instance normalization is a \emph{deterministic lossy compression}: it maps $X \mapsto S$, discarding $(M, \Sigma)$. In rate-distortion theory~\citep{cover2006elements}, this compression has rate $R_\text{strip} = I(X; M, \Sigma \mid S)$ and distortion (for routing) $D_\text{route} = H(E \mid S) - H(E \mid X) = I(M, \Sigma; E \mid S)$, which is exactly Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(i). Under Gaussianity, the signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D})$ is proportional to the rate-distortion slope at the normalization operating point: it measures how much routing capacity is consumed by the statistics that normalization strips. Specifically, if the joint $(M, \Sigma, S)$ is multivariate Gaussian, then $I(M, \Sigma; E) \propto \log(1 + R(\mathcal{D}))$ (by the Gaussian mutual information formula), making $R(\mathcal{D})$ a \emph{monotone surrogate for the bits lost to normalization}. The validated $\rho = -0.88$ correlation (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}) is thus not a statistical coincidence but a consequence of this rate-distortion structure.
```

```
61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility.
65	
66	Today, every public TSFM benchmark uses a single static head that pools hidden states and projects them to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. Real time series are not so uniform: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical: one TSFM in GPU memory can serve thousands of tenants through small ($\sim$1.7\,MB) per-task adapters swapped in microseconds, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
67	
68	This natural mixture-of-experts approach catastrophically fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) also do not help (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
69	
70	Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so frozen adapters should beat full fine-tuning, which we confirm empirically.
71	
72	\paragraph{Contributions.}
```

```
375	\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). 988	
```
```
989	\section{Experimental Setup Details}
990	\label{app:setup}
991	
992	\paragraph{Backbone specifications.} MOMENT-small ($d{=}512$, 8 encoder blocks, internal RevIN); MOMENT-large ($d{=}1024$, 24 blocks, internal RevIN); Moirai ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, LayerNorm only); Moirai-MoE ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, LayerNorm only with sparse internal experts); Chronos ($d{=}512$, 6 encoder blocks, no instance normalization); Timer-XL ($d{=}512$, 8 blocks, no instance normalization). Throughout, ``full fine-tuning'' means \emph{all} encoder blocks are unfrozen (8/8 for MOMENT-small, 24/24 for MOMENT-large, 6/6 for Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos, 8/8 for Timer-XL).
993	
994	\paragraph{Training hyperparameters.} Adam optimizer, learning rate $10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size $128$, $15$ epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Core multi-seed grids use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$); ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$ ($n{=}3$). Channel-wise StandardScaler normalization~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst} is applied to all inputs.
995	
996	\paragraph{Per-baseline configurations.}
997	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, itemsep=0.15em, topsep=0.2em]
998	\item \textbf{Fixed adapters}: linear, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool single-head topologies with $\leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters.
999	\item \textbf{LoRA}~\citep{hu2022lora}: 108-run sweep over rank, $\alpha$, and target modules; full table in Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}.
1000	\item \textbf{TRACE}~\citep{li2025trace}: importance-based LoRA selection with default settings.
1001	\item \textbf{Independent ensemble}: 5 experts with the canonical pool topologies, trained independently with no router and averaged at inference.
1002	\item \textbf{AdaMix}~\citep{wang2022adamix}: $K{=}5$ canonical heads with hidden-state softmax router; load-balance coefficient $0.01$. Full implementation details in Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}.
1003	\item \textbf{Full fine-tuning}: all encoder blocks unfrozen; best of $5$ head topologies $\times$ $2$ learning rates ($10^{-4}$, $10^{-5}$). Extended sweeps in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}.
1004	\item \textbf{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear}: from-scratch supervised; calibration values $0.416 / 0.322 / 0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather. Full table in Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}.
1005	\end{itemize}
1006	
1007	\paragraph{RR-MoA pseudocode.}
1008	\begin{algorithm}[h]
1009	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
1010	\label{alg:rrmoa}
1011	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
1012	\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
1013	\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
1014	\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
1015	\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
1016	\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
1017	\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
```

```json
  "command": "python3 << 'EOF'\nimport re, subprocess\n\nwith open('/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex') as f:\n    text = f.read()\n\n# Split main paper from appendix at \\appendix\nappendix_idx = text.find('\\\\appendix')\nmain_text = text[:appendix_idx]\nappendix_text = text[appendix_idx:]\n\n# Extract all app:XXX labels in appendix order\nlabels_with_pos = [(m.group(1), m.start()) for m in re.finditer(r'\\\\label\\{(app:[^}]+)\\}', appendix_text)]\n\n# Compute appendix letter for each by scanning sections preceding each label\nsections = [(m.start(), m.group(0)) for m in re.finditer(r'^\\\\section\\{', appendix_text, re.MULTILINE)]\ndef letter_at(pos):\n    n = sum(1 for s, _ in sections if s <= pos)\n    return chr(ord('A') + n - 1) if n > 0 else '?'\n\nprint(\"=== Appendix labels and their resolved letters ===\")\nfor lbl, pos in labels_with_pos:\n    print(f\"  {letter_at(pos)}  {lbl}\")\n\n# Find all \\ref{app:...} in main paper\nprint(\"\\n=== Main paper references to appendix ===\")\nmain_refs = re.findall(r'\\\\(?:ref|autoref|Cref)\\{(app:[^}]+)\\}', main_text)\nprint(f\"Total: {len(main_refs)} refs to {len(set(main_refs))} unique appendix labels\")\n\ndefined_labels = {lbl: letter_at(pos) for lbl, pos in labels_with_pos}\nmissing = [r for r in main_refs if r not in defined_labels]\nprint(f\"Missing/dangling: {missing if missing else 'none'}\")\n\n# Use the rendered PDF to cross-check letter assignment\nr = subprocess.run(['pdftotext', '-layout', 'main.pdf', '-'], capture_output=True, text=True)\npdf_text = r.stdout\n\n# Find all \"Appendix X[.Y]\" occurrences in main paper portion\n# Find page where conclusion is, take everything before\npages = pdf_text.split('\\x0c')\nmain_pdf_pages = []\nfor p in pages:\n    main_pdf_pages.append(p)\n    if 'Conclusion and Limitations' in p:\n        break\n\nmain_pdf_text = '\\n'.join(main_pdf_pages)\nappendix_refs_in_pdf = re.findall(r'Appendix\\s+([A-Z](?:\\.\\d+)?)', main_pdf_text)\nprint(f\"\\n=== Rendered 'Appendix X' citations in main paper PDF ===\")\nprint(f\"Distinct: {sorted(set(appendix_refs_in_pdf))}\")\n\n# Also look for 'Appendices A and B' style\nmulti = re.findall(r'Appendices?\\s+([A-Z](?:\\.\\d+)?(?:[\\s,]+(?:and\\s+)?[A-Z](?:\\.\\d+)?)*)', main_pdf_text)\nprint(f\"Appendices ranges: {multi[:10]}\")\nEOF",
```

```
1869	\textbf{Key findings.} (1)~Normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy across all nine datasets, confirming that RevIN universally degrades routing signal. (2)~The bound gap $I(M,\Sigma; S \mid E)$ averages $0.075$ nats ($4.7\%$ of $\log K$), so the lower bound is empirically tight. (3)~Crucially, $H(E \mid S)$ does \emph{not} correlate with $\Delta\%$ ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$, $p{=}0.77$), while $R(\mathcal{D})$ does ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$). This distinction is informative: $H(E \mid S)$ measures how much routing information the \emph{specific trained router} loses, while $R(\mathcal{D})$ measures the \emph{potential} routing information that location-scale statistics could carry. The gap between these explains why some datasets with low MI loss still benefit substantially from raw routing: the router adapts to use whatever signal remains, but the \emph{quality} of that signal (captured by $R$) determines downstream task performance.
1870	
1871	\textbf{Synthetic validation.} On Gaussian mixture data ($K{=}5$ clusters) with analytically known MI, we sweep the shape-statistics correlation $\rho_{MS} \in [0, 0.9]$. At $\rho_{MS}{=}0$, the bound is tight (gap $= 0.19$ nats, $12\%$ of $\log K$). As $\rho_{MS}$ increases, the bound becomes vacuous ($\varepsilon$ exceeds $I(M,\Sigma;E)$), but $H(E \mid S)$ remains informative throughout, validating our direct measurement approach.
1872
```

```
1892	\section{Information Content Diagnostic}
1893	\label{app:diagnostic}
1894	
1895	To quantify the information bottleneck created by RevIN and the encoder, we compare the forecasting value of backbone features against raw input using non-parametric methods. All experiments run on CPU with zero GPU cost.
1896	
1897	\begin{table}[h]
1898	\centering
1899	\caption{\textbf{Backbone features vs.\ raw input} (H=96, 80/20 train/test split). k-NN: k-nearest neighbor regression ($k{=}20$, distance-weighted) on mean-pooled backbone features vs.\ raw 512-dim input. Ridge: regularized linear regression (cross-validated $\alpha$). Backbone features are $16$--$33\%$ worse than raw input even with a universal approximator (k-NN), confirming genuine information destruction.}
1900	\label{tab:diagnostic}
1901	\small
1902	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}

1904	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{k-NN (MSE)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Ridge (MSE)} & Backbone \\
1905	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
1906	Dataset & Raw input & Backbone & Raw input & Backbone & penalty \\
1907	\midrule
1908	ETTh1   & $0.636$ & $0.737$ & $0.551$ & $1.230$ & $+16\%$ \\
1909	ETTm1   & $0.498$ & $0.614$ & $0.363$ & $0.708$ & $+23\%$ \\

1914	
1915	Residual correction experiments further confirm that backbone features provide negligible complementary signal: adding backbone features (via Ridge or Random Forest) as a residual correction atop a linear raw-input predictor improves MSE by at most $0.55\%$. On ETTh1, Random Forest regression on raw input alone reduces MSE by $44\%$ versus Ridge, revealing substantial nonlinear temporal structure in the raw data. However, Random Forest on backbone features adds only $+0.02\%$ to the linear baseline: the encoder destroys this nonlinear structure entirely. Few-shot experiments (Table~\ref{tab:fewshot}) confirm the pattern holds at every training set size $N \in \{10, \ldots, 5000\}$: DLinear dominates at all $N$, ruling out data scarcity as a regime where the frozen backbone recovers its advantage. The gap to DLinear reflects information destruction by the normalization--encoding pipeline, not a limitation of adapter design or training data volume.
```

```
2095	\textbf{Causal proof via RevIN ablation.} Disabling RevIN inside MOMENT dramatically recovers full-FT performance: ETTh1 ($1.063 \to 0.490$, $-54\%$), ETTm1 ($0.910 \to 0.364$, $-60\%$), Weather ($0.481 \to 0.187$, $-61\%$). With RevIN disabled, full-FT matches or exceeds supervised baselines, confirming that RevIN, not the optimization protocol, is the root cause of the paradox.
2096	
2097	\textbf{Adapter training budget.} Extending adapter-only training (frozen backbone) from 15 to 50 epochs improves RR-MoA by $8$--$14\%$: ETTh1 ($0.690 \to 0.633$), ETTm1 ($0.572 \to 0.494$), Weather ($0.289 \to 0.250$). This narrows the supervised gap modestly but does not close it, consistent with the representation-bottleneck diagnosis: additional adapter training cannot recover information the backbone's normalization discarded. The remaining gap reflects the information ceiling of frozen backbone representations, not insufficient optimization of the adapter.
2098	
2099	\section{Closing the DLinear Gap}
2100	\label{app:gap_closing}
2101	\label{app:gap_closing_ext}
2102	
2103	A good diagnosis should be \emph{actionable}: identifying the root cause of a performance gap should point directly to how to close it. Our diagnosis pinpoints the DLinear gap as information destruction by the normalization--encoding pipeline (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}): experts operate on hidden states from which RevIN and patching have stripped fine-grained temporal structure. This diagnosis makes a clear architectural prediction: \emph{if we restore raw-signal access to the experts, the gap should close.} We test this prediction with three architectures.
2104	
2105	We test three architectures that provide experts with raw-input access while preserving the frozen-backbone paradigm and RR-MoA routing:
2106	
2107	\textbf{(1)~Dual-Stream.} Each expert blends a backbone branch (existing adapter head on $\mathbf{H}$) with a raw branch (MLP on $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$) via a learnable per-expert scalar $\alpha_k$: $\mathbf{y}_k = \sigma(\alpha_k)\cdot\text{Adapter}_k(\mathbf{H}) + (1{-}\sigma(\alpha_k))\cdot\text{MLP}_k(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$.
2108	
2109	\textbf{(2)~Raw-Input Expert.} A 6th expert operating directly on $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ (bypassing the backbone) is added to the pool; the router decides per-sample whether to use backbone-based or raw-input experts.
```

```
2553	
2554	The pattern is clear: (1)~at H${=}96$, \textbf{every backbone wins on every dataset} ($27/27$); (2)~MOMENT-large achieves $\mathbf{23/24}$ ($96\%$) with $16$ sig wins and zero losses; (3)~Moirai-MoE achieves $\mathbf{15/15}$ ($100\%$), confirming adapter-level routing stacks with backbone-internal MoE; (4)~Moirai at $15/24$ ($62\%$) is the weakest, driven by ETTh2 long-horizon instability and Electricity losses. The recipe transfers across all five normalization regimes and architectures.
2555	
2556	\subsection{SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+}: Self-Routed Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}}
2557	\label{app:sr_ria}
2558	
2559	SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} combines two independently validated components: (i)~SR-MoA self-routing, where each expert has its own sigmoid gate on raw input (no external router; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}), and (ii)~Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}'s dual-stream expert architecture (shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual; Table~\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus}). Each expert $k$ produces $\mathbf{y}_k = \text{NLinear}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}) + \sigma(\text{blend}_k(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})) \cdot \text{Adapter}_k(\mathbf{H})$, where the NLinear branch is shared across experts and the blend gate is initialized at $b{=}{-}2$ ($\sigma(-2){\approx}0.12$). Training uses 5-epoch raw-branch warmup, cosine LR decay, and validation-based early stopping with patience 5.

2582	\subsection{Pure Raw-MLP MoE Ablation: Quantifying the TSFM's Contribution}
2583	\label{app:raw_mlp_moe}
2584	
2585	A natural concern about the dual-stream gap-closing result is that it could be confounded: if the TSFM destroys nonlinear temporal structure (Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), and the dual-stream fix recovers that structure via the raw branch, perhaps the raw branch is doing all the work and the TSFM is dead weight. We tested this hypothesis directly by running a pure mixture-of-experts ablation in which the TSFM is removed entirely.
```

```
1008:\item \textbf{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear}: from-scratch supervised; calibration values $0.416 / 0.322 / 0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather. Full table in Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}.
1161:The first term (self-gradient) always decreases $\ell_1$. The second (cross-gradient) is negligible when expert gradients are approximately orthogonal, a condition that holds empirically once experts specialize to distinct input clusters (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_weights}). The third (loss-coupling) term is small early in training when $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| \ll \|\partial\ell_1/\partial\mathbf{h}\|$. Retaining only the dominant first term:
1473:\textbf{Instance-adaptive gating (IA-Gating).} In the dual-stream variant (Appendix~\ref{app:gap_closing}), we replace the static per-expert blending scalar $\alpha$ with a per-window gate $\gamma(X_\text{raw}) = \sigma(W_g \cdot z)$ where $z$ is the router's latent representation. Across 6 datasets (frozen, 3 seeds), IA-Gating wins on 4/6 (ETTh2 $-8.0\%$, ETTm2 $-6.4\%$, ETTm1 $-1.9\%$, Electricity $-0.4\%$) and loses on 2/6 (Weather $+6.1\%$, ETTh1 $+0.3\%$). The learned gates cluster tightly around $\gamma \approx 0.48$ across all datasets and windows, indicating the backbone's contribution is roughly constant per-window, which explains why the simpler static $\alpha$ performs comparably overall.
2045:\label{app:gap_closing}
2046:\label{app:gap_closing_ext}
```

```
543	
544	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}, Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), confirming the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\,\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a \emph{phase transition} (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs): MSE degrades mildly for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ ($+5.4\%$) but catastrophically at $\alpha{=}1.0$ ($+283\%$), while entropy stays in $[1.43, 1.57]$: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth}). A temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
545	
546	
547	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} The experiments above converge on a single mechanism: instance normalization strips the per-window statistics that the router uses to tell input regimes apart. To pin this down, we ask what information the router actually has access to before and after normalization. Each input window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into a mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, a scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and a shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$. RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$, so the question reduces to how much routing information lives in the discarded part. The mutual-information chain rule gives a clean answer.
```

```
542	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). As the diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes this gap: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert's output; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) matches or beats DLinear on \textbf{6/6 datasets} at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$), generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones.
```
